========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m here because I… I keep blowing up at people, and it’s scaring me a little. I work in retail, and I’m used to things being chaotic, people making mistakes, shelves being wrong, customers being difficult — that’s normal, I can usually handle that. But lately I’ve been snapping over things that honestly are not that big.

Like the other day, one of my employees put some stock on the wrong shelf, and I just yelled. Not corrected them, not talked to them — I yelled. And afterward I was standing there shaking, like my whole body was buzzing, because that is not how I usually treat people. I kept thinking, “Why did I do that? Why was I so harsh?” It was such a small thing, and I reacted like it was some huge disaster. I don’t want to be that kind of person or that kind of manager. That’s basically why I came in — I feel like I can’t trust my reactions right now.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. Very much, honestly. I feel like my nerves are just… sitting right under my skin all the time.

At work especially, it’s embarrassing. If one of the store alarms beeps, or if someone bangs a pallet jack behind me, I jump so hard I almost drop whatever I’m holding. And it’s not just a little flinch, it’s like my whole body reacts before I even understand what the sound was. Then my heart keeps pounding afterward, even when everyone else has already gone back to what they were doing like nothing happened.

I’ll be standing there trying to act normal, like, “Okay, keep scanning, keep stocking, keep talking,” but inside I’m still rattled. It feels like my body thinks something terrible just happened, even when it’s just a normal store noise.

Not corrected them, not talked to them — I yelled. And afterward I was standing there shaking, like my whole body was buzzing, because that is not how I usually treat people. I kept thinking, “Why did I do that? Why was I so harsh?” It was such a small thing, and I reacted like it was some huge disaster. I don’t want to be that kind of person or that kind of manager. That’s basically why I came in — I feel like I can’t trust my reactions right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge more often than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much, honestly. I feel like my nerves are just… sitting right under my skin all the time.\n\nAt work especially, it’s embarrassing. If one of the store alarms beeps, or if someone bangs a pallet jack behind me, I jump so hard I almost drop whatever I’m holding. And it’s not just a little flinch, it’s like my whole body reacts before I even understand what the sound was. Then my heart keeps pounding afterward, even when everyone else has already gone back to what they were doing like nothing happened.\n\nI’ll be standing there trying to act normal, like, “Okay, keep scanning, keep stocking, keep talking,” but inside I’m still rattled. It feels like my body thinks something terrible just happened, even when it’s just a normal store noise.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Um… I don’t think so? At least not in the way you mean, I don’t think.

I mean, I do get stuck on things in my head sometimes, but it doesn’t feel like rituals, like I have to touch something a certain number of times or check the door over and over or wash my hands repeatedly. I’m not doing anything like that, as far as I’ve noticed.

If you mean do I keep replaying situations in my head, then maybe, but not because I feel like I have to do some specific action to make it stop. It’s more like my mind just won’t let something go. But no, I don’t have any particular routines or rules that I feel forced to follow.

I kept thinking, “Why did I do that? Why was I so harsh?” It was such a small thing, and I reacted like it was some huge disaster. I don’t want to be that kind of person or that kind of manager. That’s basically why I came in — I feel like I can’t trust my reactions right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge more often than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much, honestly. I feel like my nerves are just… sitting right under my skin all the time.\n\nAt work especially, it’s embarrassing. If one of the store alarms beeps, or if someone bangs a pallet jack behind me, I jump so hard I almost drop whatever I’m holding. And it’s not just a little flinch, it’s like my whole body reacts before I even understand what the sound was. Then my heart keeps pounding afterward, even when everyone else has already gone back to what they were doing like nothing happened.\n\nI’ll be standing there trying to act normal, like, “Okay, keep scanning, keep stocking, keep talking,” but inside I’m still rattled. It feels like my body thinks something terrible just happened, even when it’s just a normal store noise.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts or urges to perform specific rituals that you feel compelled to follow?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? At least not in the way you mean, I don’t think.\n\nI mean, I do get stuck on things in my head sometimes, but it doesn’t feel like rituals, like I have to touch something a certain number of times or check the door over and over or wash my hands repeatedly. I’m not doing anything like that, as far as I’ve noticed.\n\nIf you mean do I keep replaying situations in my head, then maybe, but not because I feel like I have to do some specific action to make it stop. It’s more like my mind just won’t let something go. But no, I don’t have any particular routines or rules that I feel forced to follow.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 40 / retail_salesperson / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I… I don’t know that I’d call it sad most of the time. Not exactly.

I mean, I’m upset with myself a lot, and I feel worn down, but when you say sad or down, I picture crying all day or feeling hopeless, and I’m not sure that’s what this is. I can still get through work, I still do what I’m supposed to do, but I don’t feel like myself. It’s more like I’m tense and frustrated and then afterward I feel guilty, like, “What is wrong with me?”

So… maybe some down moments, yes, especially after I’ve snapped at someone or embarrassed myself, but I haven’t really been thinking of it as sadness most of the time. It feels more like I’m overwhelmed and not handling things the way I normally would.

Yes. I have been, and I didn’t even realize how much until I started noticing all the little ways I’m arranging my day around it.

The house is the big one. I keep finding excuses not to go near it, or not to deal with anything connected to it. Even just thinking about going over there makes my chest tighten, like my body is saying, “No, don’t.” It sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, because it’s just a place, but it doesn’t feel like just a place right now.

And there’s this mortgage folder I need to deal with, papers and documents and all of that, and I can’t make myself open it. I’ll put it on the table and then move it somewhere else, or tell myself I’ll look at it after dinner, and then suddenly it’s bedtime and I haven’t touched it. I know it’s important. I know avoiding it probably makes things worse. But anything tied to that day just makes me feel like I can’t breathe right.

Even the herbs on my patio… I’ve been avoiding those too. That sounds so small, I know, but I used to go out there and water them without thinking, and now I’ll see them through the glass and just turn away. It’s like they’re connected to all of it somehow, and my chest gets tight before I even step outside. I just can’t make myself go near things that remind me of what happened.

Very much, honestly. I feel like my nerves are just… sitting right under my skin all the time.\n\nAt work especially, it’s embarrassing. If one of the store alarms beeps, or if someone bangs a pallet jack behind me, I jump so hard I almost drop whatever I’m holding. And it’s not just a little flinch, it’s like my whole body reacts before I even understand what the sound was. Then my heart keeps pounding afterward, even when everyone else has already gone back to what they were doing like nothing happened.\n\nI’ll be standing there trying to act normal, like, “Okay, keep scanning, keep stocking, keep talking,” but inside I’m still rattled. It feels like my body thinks something terrible just happened, even when it’s just a normal store noise.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts or urges to perform specific rituals that you feel compelled to follow?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? At least not in the way you mean, I don’t think.\n\nI mean, I do get stuck on things in my head sometimes, but it doesn’t feel like rituals, like I have to touch something a certain number of times or check the door over and over or wash my hands repeatedly. I’m not doing anything like that, as far as I’ve noticed.\n\nIf you mean do I keep replaying situations in my head, then maybe, but not because I feel like I have to do some specific action to make it stop. It’s more like my mind just won’t let something go. But no, I don’t have any particular routines or rules that I feel forced to follow.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know that I’d call it sad most of the time. Not exactly.\n\nI mean, I’m upset with myself a lot, and I feel worn down, but when you say sad or down, I picture crying all day or feeling hopeless, and I’m not sure that’s what this is. I can still get through work, I still do what I’m supposed to do, but I don’t feel like myself. It’s more like I’m tense and frustrated and then afterward I feel guilty, like, “What is wrong with me?”\n\nSo… maybe some down moments, yes, especially after I’ve snapped at someone or embarrassed myself, but I haven’t really been thinking of it as sadness most of the time. It feels more like I’m overwhelmed and not handling things the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain situations or places because of how they make you feel?\n\nPatient: Yes. I have been, and I didn’t even realize how much until I started noticing all the little ways I’m arranging my day around it.\n\nThe house is the big one. I keep finding excuses not to go near it, or not to deal with anything connected to it. Even just thinking about going over there makes my chest tighten, like my body is saying, “No, don’t.” It sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, because it’s just a place, but it doesn’t feel like just a place right now.\n\nAnd there’s this mortgage folder I need to deal with, papers and documents and all of that, and I can’t make myself open it. I’ll put it on the table and then move it somewhere else, or tell myself I’ll look at it after dinner, and then suddenly it’s bedtime and I haven’t touched it. I know it’s important. I know avoiding it probably makes things worse. But anything tied to that day just makes me feel like I can’t breathe right.\n\nEven the herbs on my patio… I’ve been avoiding those too. That sounds so small, I know, but I used to go out there and water them without thinking, and now I’ll see them through the glass and just turn away. It’s like they’re connected to all of it somehow, and my chest gets tight before I even step outside. I just can’t make myself go near things that remind me of what happened.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… something happened that day, and now it’s like my brain has attached everything around it to the house.

I don’t even have to be there. Just thinking about the house, or seeing that mortgage folder sitting there, or even looking out at the herbs on my patio — things that should be totally ordinary — my chest tightens up and I just sort of freeze. I tell myself, “Just go deal with it, just open the folder, just water the plants,” and then I can’t. I move around it. I make excuses. I put it off until later.

It’s not that those things are dangerous by themselves. I know that logically. But they feel tied to that day, and once that feeling hits, I just want to get away from it. So I’ve been avoiding the house, the paperwork, even the patio, because anything connected to it makes me feel like I can’t get close to it.

I’ll put it on the table and then move it somewhere else, or tell myself I’ll look at it after dinner, and then suddenly it’s bedtime and I haven’t touched it. I know it’s important. I know avoiding it probably makes things worse. But anything tied to that day just makes me feel like I can’t breathe right.\n\nEven the herbs on my patio… I’ve been avoiding those too. That sounds so small, I know, but I used to go out there and water them without thinking, and now I’ll see them through the glass and just turn away. It’s like they’re connected to all of it somehow, and my chest gets tight before I even step outside. I just can’t make myself go near things that remind me of what happened.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen that you're associating with the house or the things you're avoiding?\n\nPatient: Yes… something happened that day, and now it’s like my brain has attached everything around it to the house.\n\nI don’t even have to be there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I mean… not like I’m literally standing there every second, but it comes in so fast that it feels like I’m back in it before I even understand what’s happening.

The fire just jumps into my head out of nowhere. I can be doing something completely normal, like picking up a pan in the kitchen, and suddenly it’s not just a pan anymore — I’m smelling smoke again, and I can see the kitchen filling up, like that moment is happening right in front of me. And it’s so vivid for a few seconds that I’m not thinking, “Oh, this is a memory.” I’m just back there, trying to make sense of it and stop it, and then I realize I’m standing in my own kitchen now, or wherever I am, and I feel shaken.

It’s happened with things that don’t even seem connected at first. Like I’ll open a spreadsheet at work, just some ordinary thing I need to do, and then my mind will suddenly throw me right back to that day — the smoke, the kitchen, that awful feeling of everything happening too fast. I hate it because there’s no warning. I’ll be fine, or at least trying to be fine, and then there it is, like my brain yanks me back into it before I can stop it.

I just can’t make myself go near things that remind me of what happened.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen that you're associating with the house or the things you're avoiding?\n\nPatient: Yes… something happened that day, and now it’s like my brain has attached everything around it to the house.\n\nI don’t even have to be there. Just thinking about the house, or seeing that mortgage folder sitting there, or even looking out at the herbs on my patio — things that should be totally ordinary — my chest tightens up and I just sort of freeze. I tell myself, “Just go deal with it, just open the folder, just water the plants,” and then I can’t. I move around it. I make excuses. I put it off until later.\n\nIt’s not that those things are dangerous by themselves. I know that logically. But they feel tied to that day, and once that feeling hits, I just want to get away from it. So I’ve been avoiding the house, the paperwork, even the patio, because anything connected to it makes me feel like I can’t get close to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself reliving that specific day or event as if it were happening again?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… not like I’m literally standing there every second, but it comes in so fast that it feels like I’m back in it before I even understand what’s happening.\n\nThe fire just jumps into my head out of nowhere. I can be doing something completely normal, like picking up a pan in the kitchen, and suddenly it’s not just a pan anymore — I’m smelling smoke again, and I can see the kitchen filling up, like that moment is happening right in front of me. And it’s so vivid for a few seconds that I’m not thinking, “Oh, this is a memory.” I’m just back there, trying to make sense of it and stop it, and then I realize I’m standing in my own kitchen now, or wherever I am, and I feel shaken.\n\nIt’s happened with things that don’t even seem connected at first. Like I’ll open a spreadsheet at work, just some ordinary thing I need to do, and then my mind will suddenly throw me right back to that day — the smoke, the kitchen, that awful feeling of everything happening too fast. I hate it because there’s no warning. I’ll be fine, or at least trying to be fine, and then there it is, like my brain yanks me back into it before I can stop it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… mostly the same few things, but they sort of spread out into my day more than I want to admit.

I avoid the house the most. If I have to go near it or even think about dealing with something there, I start feeling it in my chest right away, like it tightens before I’ve even made a decision. I’ll tell myself I’m just too busy, or that I’ll go later, or that someone else can handle it, but really I know I’m trying not to feel that feeling.

And the mortgage folder — I know I’ve mentioned it, but that one is almost embarrassing because it’s just paperwork. It’s sitting there, and I’ll walk past it like it’s something alive. I can’t seem to open it because it pulls me right back to everything connected to that day. I’ll move it from one spot to another instead of actually looking at it.

Even the herbs on my patio. That sounds so small, but I’ve been avoiding them too. I used to just step outside and water them, check on them, whatever. Now I see them through the door and I just turn away. Anything that feels tied to that day makes my body react before I can talk myself through it. My chest gets tight, and I just can’t make myself go near it.

At least not in the way you mean, I don’t think.\n\nI mean, I do get stuck on things in my head sometimes, but it doesn’t feel like rituals, like I have to touch something a certain number of times or check the door over and over or wash my hands repeatedly. I’m not doing anything like that, as far as I’ve noticed.\n\nIf you mean do I keep replaying situations in my head, then maybe, but not because I feel like I have to do some specific action to make it stop. It’s more like my mind just won’t let something go. But no, I don’t have any particular routines or rules that I feel forced to follow.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know that I’d call it sad most of the time. Not exactly.\n\nI mean, I’m upset with myself a lot, and I feel worn down, but when you say sad or down, I picture crying all day or feeling hopeless, and I’m not sure that’s what this is. I can still get through work, I still do what I’m supposed to do, but I don’t feel like myself. It’s more like I’m tense and frustrated and then afterward I feel guilty, like, “What is wrong with me?”\n\nSo… maybe some down moments, yes, especially after I’ve snapped at someone or embarrassed myself, but I haven’t really been thinking of it as sadness most of the time. It feels more like I’m overwhelmed and not handling things the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain situations or places because of how they make you feel?\n\nPatient: Yes. I have been, and I didn’t even realize how much until I started noticing all the little ways I’m arranging my day around it.\n\nThe house is the big one. I keep finding excuses not to go near it, or not to deal with anything connected to it. Even just thinking about going over there makes my chest tighten, like my body is saying, “No, don’t.” It sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, because it’s just a place, but it doesn’t feel like just a place right now.\n\nAnd there’s this mortgage folder I need to deal with, papers and documents and all of that, and I can’t make myself open it. I’ll put it on the table and then move it somewhere else, or tell myself I’ll look at it after dinner, and then suddenly it’s bedtime and I haven’t touched it. I know it’s important. I know avoiding it probably makes things worse. But anything tied to that day just makes me feel like I can’t breathe right.\n\nEven the herbs on my patio… I’ve been avoiding those too. That sounds so small, I know, but I used to go out there and water them without thinking, and now I’ll see them through the glass and just turn away. It’s like they’re connected to all of it somehow, and my chest gets tight before I even step outside. I just can’t make myself go near things that remind me of what happened.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen that you're associating with the house or the things you're avoiding?\n\nPatient: Yes… something happened that day, and now it’s like my brain has attached everything around it to the house.\n\nI don’t even have to be there. Just thinking about the house, or seeing that mortgage folder sitting there, or even looking out at the herbs on my patio — things that should be totally ordinary — my chest tightens up and I just sort of freeze. I tell myself, “Just go deal with it, just open the folder, just water the plants,” and then I can’t. I move around it. I make excuses. I put it off until later.\n\nIt’s not that those things are dangerous by themselves. I know that logically. But they feel tied to that day, and once that feeling hits, I just want to get away from it. So I’ve been avoiding the house, the paperwork, even the patio, because anything connected to it makes me feel like I can’t get close to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself reliving that specific day or event as if it were happening again?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… not like I’m literally standing there every second, but it comes in so fast that it feels like I’m back in it before I even understand what’s happening.\n\nThe fire just jumps into my head out of nowhere. I can be doing something completely normal, like picking up a pan in the kitchen, and suddenly it’s not just a pan anymore — I’m smelling smoke again, and I can see the kitchen filling up, like that moment is happening right in front of me. And it’s so vivid for a few seconds that I’m not thinking, “Oh, this is a memory.” I’m just back there, trying to make sense of it and stop it, and then I realize I’m standing in my own kitchen now, or wherever I am, and I feel shaken.\n\nIt’s happened with things that don’t even seem connected at first. Like I’ll open a spreadsheet at work, just some ordinary thing I need to do, and then my mind will suddenly throw me right back to that day — the smoke, the kitchen, that awful feeling of everything happening too fast. I hate it because there’s no warning. I’ll be fine, or at least trying to be fine, and then there it is, like my brain yanks me back into it before I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other places, activities, or things that you've been avoiding because they remind you of the fire?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly the same few things, but they sort of spread out into my day more than I want to admit.\n\nI avoid the house the most. If I have to go near it or even think about dealing with something there, I start feeling it in my chest right away, like it tightens before I’ve even made a decision. I’ll tell myself I’m just too busy, or that I’ll go later, or that someone else can handle it, but really I know I’m trying not to feel that feeling.\n\nAnd the mortgage folder — I know I’ve mentioned it, but that one is almost embarrassing because it’s just paperwork. It’s sitting there, and I’ll walk past it like it’s something alive. I can’t seem to open it because it pulls me right back to everything connected to that day. I’ll move it from one spot to another instead of actually looking at it.\n\nEven the herbs on my patio. That sounds so small, but I’ve been avoiding them too. I used to just step outside and water them, check on them, whatever. Now I see them through the door and I just turn away. Anything that feels tied to that day makes my body react before I can talk myself through it. My chest gets tight, and I just can’t make myself go near it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think that’s a big part of it. It’s not like I sit there and calmly decide, “I’m afraid I’ll lose control,” but that’s what it feels like underneath. Like if I go to the house, or if I open that mortgage folder, or even if I step out to deal with the herbs on the patio, something in me just clamps down. My chest gets tight right away, and I feel like if I keep going toward it, I’m going to get overwhelmed and not be able to pull myself back together.

So I avoid it. I walk around the folder. I look away from the patio. I put off anything connected to the house. And I know those things aren’t the fire itself, I know they’re just objects or places, but they’re tied to that day in my mind, and once that feeling starts, I just… I can’t make myself go near them. It feels safer to stay away than to risk having that reaction take over.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.0, F43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I… I keep blowing up at people, and it’s scaring me a little. I work in retail, and I’m used to things being chaotic, people making mistakes, shelves being wrong, customers being difficult — that’s normal, I can usually handle that. But lately I’ve been snapping over things that honestly are not that big.\n\nLike the other day, one of my employees put some stock on the wrong shelf, and I just yelled. Not corrected them, not talked to them — I yelled. And afterward I was standing there shaking, like my whole body was buzzing, because that is not how I usually treat people. I kept thinking, “Why did I do that? Why was I so harsh?” It was such a small thing, and I reacted like it was some huge disaster. I don’t want to be that kind of person or that kind of manager. That’s basically why I came in — I feel like I can’t trust my reactions right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge more often than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much, honestly. I feel like my nerves are just… sitting right under my skin all the time.\n\nAt work especially, it’s embarrassing. If one of the store alarms beeps, or if someone bangs a pallet jack behind me, I jump so hard I almost drop whatever I’m holding. And it’s not just a little flinch, it’s like my whole body reacts before I even understand what the sound was. Then my heart keeps pounding afterward, even when everyone else has already gone back to what they were doing like nothing happened.\n\nI’ll be standing there trying to act normal, like, “Okay, keep scanning, keep stocking, keep talking,” but inside I’m still rattled. It feels like my body thinks something terrible just happened, even when it’s just a normal store noise.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts or urges to perform specific rituals that you feel compelled to follow?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? At least not in the way you mean, I don’t think.\n\nI mean, I do get stuck on things in my head sometimes, but it doesn’t feel like rituals, like I have to touch something a certain number of times or check the door over and over or wash my hands repeatedly. I’m not doing anything like that, as far as I’ve noticed.\n\nIf you mean do I keep replaying situations in my head, then maybe, but not because I feel like I have to do some specific action to make it stop. It’s more like my mind just won’t let something go. But no, I don’t have any particular routines or rules that I feel forced to follow.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know that I’d call it sad most of the time. Not exactly.\n\nI mean, I’m upset with myself a lot, and I feel worn down, but when you say sad or down, I picture crying all day or feeling hopeless, and I’m not sure that’s what this is. I can still get through work, I still do what I’m supposed to do, but I don’t feel like myself. It’s more like I’m tense and frustrated and then afterward I feel guilty, like, “What is wrong with me?”\n\nSo… maybe some down moments, yes, especially after I’ve snapped at someone or embarrassed myself, but I haven’t really been thinking of it as sadness most of the time. It feels more like I’m overwhelmed and not handling things the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain situations or places because of how they make you feel?\n\nPatient: Yes. I have been, and I didn’t even realize how much until I started noticing all the little ways I’m arranging my day around it.\n\nThe house is the big one. I keep finding excuses not to go near it, or not to deal with anything connected to it. Even just thinking about going over there makes my chest tighten, like my body is saying, “No, don’t.” It sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, because it’s just a place, but it doesn’t feel like just a place right now.\n\nAnd there’s this mortgage folder I need to deal with, papers and documents and all of that, and I can’t make myself open it. I’ll put it on the table and then move it somewhere else, or tell myself I’ll look at it after dinner, and then suddenly it’s bedtime and I haven’t touched it. I know it’s important. I know avoiding it probably makes things worse. But anything tied to that day just makes me feel like I can’t breathe right.\n\nEven the herbs on my patio… I’ve been avoiding those too. That sounds so small, I know, but I used to go out there and water them without thinking, and now I’ll see them through the glass and just turn away. It’s like they’re connected to all of it somehow, and my chest gets tight before I even step outside. I just can’t make myself go near things that remind me of what happened.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen that you're associating with the house or the things you're avoiding?\n\nPatient: Yes… something happened that day, and now it’s like my brain has attached everything around it to the house.\n\nI don’t even have to be there. Just thinking about the house, or seeing that mortgage folder sitting there, or even looking out at the herbs on my patio — things that should be totally ordinary — my chest tightens up and I just sort of freeze. I tell myself, “Just go deal with it, just open the folder, just water the plants,” and then I can’t. I move around it. I make excuses. I put it off until later.\n\nIt’s not that those things are dangerous by themselves. I know that logically. But they feel tied to that day, and once that feeling hits, I just want to get away from it. So I’ve been avoiding the house, the paperwork, even the patio, because anything connected to it makes me feel like I can’t get close to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself reliving that specific day or event as if it were happening again?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… not like I’m literally standing there every second, but it comes in so fast that it feels like I’m back in it before I even understand what’s happening.\n\nThe fire just jumps into my head out of nowhere. I can be doing something completely normal, like picking up a pan in the kitchen, and suddenly it’s not just a pan anymore — I’m smelling smoke again, and I can see the kitchen filling up, like that moment is happening right in front of me. And it’s so vivid for a few seconds that I’m not thinking, “Oh, this is a memory.” I’m just back there, trying to make sense of it and stop it, and then I realize I’m standing in my own kitchen now, or wherever I am, and I feel shaken.\n\nIt’s happened with things that don’t even seem connected at first. Like I’ll open a spreadsheet at work, just some ordinary thing I need to do, and then my mind will suddenly throw me right back to that day — the smoke, the kitchen, that awful feeling of everything happening too fast. I hate it because there’s no warning. I’ll be fine, or at least trying to be fine, and then there it is, like my brain yanks me back into it before I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other places, activities, or things that you've been avoiding because they remind you of the fire?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly the same few things, but they sort of spread out into my day more than I want to admit.\n\nI avoid the house the most. If I have to go near it or even think about dealing with something there, I start feeling it in my chest right away, like it tightens before I’ve even made a decision. I’ll tell myself I’m just too busy, or that I’ll go later, or that someone else can handle it, but really I know I’m trying not to feel that feeling.\n\nAnd the mortgage folder — I know I’ve mentioned it, but that one is almost embarrassing because it’s just paperwork. It’s sitting there, and I’ll walk past it like it’s something alive. I can’t seem to open it because it pulls me right back to everything connected to that day. I’ll move it from one spot to another instead of actually looking at it.\n\nEven the herbs on my patio. That sounds so small, but I’ve been avoiding them too. I used to just step outside and water them, check on them, whatever. Now I see them through the door and I just turn away. Anything that feels tied to that day makes my body react before I can talk myself through it. My chest gets tight, and I just can’t make myself go near it.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding these situations or places because you're afraid of losing control of your emotions or reactions?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a big part of it. It’s not like I sit there and calmly decide, “I’m afraid I’ll lose control,” but that’s what it feels like underneath. Like if I go to the house, or if I open that mortgage folder, or even if I step out to deal with the herbs on the patio, something in me just clamps down. My chest gets tight right away, and I feel like if I keep going toward it, I’m going to get overwhelmed and not be able to pull myself back together.\n\nSo I avoid it. I walk around the folder. I look away from the patio. I put off anything connected to the house. And I know those things aren’t the fire itself, I know they’re just objects or places, but they’re tied to that day in my mind, and once that feeling starts, I just… I can’t make myself go near them. It feels safer to stay away than to risk having that reaction take over.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. My chest gets tight, and I just can’t make myself go near it.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding these situations or places because you're afraid of losing control of your emotions or reactions?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a big part of it. It’s not like I sit there and calmly decide, “I’m afraid I’ll lose control,” but that’s what it feels like underneath. Like if I go to the house, or if I open that mortgage folder, or even if I step out to deal with the herbs on the patio, something in me just clamps down. My chest gets tight right away, and I feel like if I keep going toward it, I’m going to get overwhelmed and not be able to pull myself back together.\n\nSo I avoid it. I walk around the folder. I look away from the patio. I put off anything connected to the house. And I know those things aren’t the fire itself, I know they’re just objects or places, but they’re tied to that day in my mind, and once that feeling starts, I just… I can’t make myself go near them. It feels safer to stay away than to risk having that reaction take over.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you have these intense reactions, do you ever feel like you're having a heart attack or going to pass out?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 10\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."

    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Acute Stress Disorder, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself reliving that specific day or event as if it were happening again?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… not like I’m literally standing there every second, but it comes in so fast that it feels like I’m back in it before I even understand what’s happening.\n\nThe fire just jumps into my head out of nowhere. I can be doing something completely normal, like picking up a pan in the kitchen, and suddenly it’s not just a pan anymore — I’m smelling smoke again, and I can see the kitchen filling up, like that moment is happening right in front of me. And it’s so vivid for a few seconds that I’m not thinking, “Oh, this is a memory.” I’m just back there, trying to make sense of it and stop it, and then I realize I’m standing in my own kitchen now, or wherever I am, and I feel shaken.\n\nIt’s happened with things that don’t even seem connected at first. Like I’ll open a spreadsheet at work, just some ordinary thing I need to do, and then my mind will suddenly throw me right back to that day — the smoke, the kitchen, that awful feeling of everything happening too fast. I hate it because there’s no warning. I’ll be fine, or at least trying to be fine, and then there it is, like my brain yanks me back into it before I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other places, activities, or things that you've been avoiding because they remind you of the fire?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly the same few things, but they sort of spread out into my day more than I want to admit.\n\nI avoid the house the most. If I have to go near it or even think about dealing with something there, I start feeling it in my chest right away, like it tightens before I’ve even made a decision. I’ll tell myself I’m just too busy, or that I’ll go later, or that someone else can handle it, but really I know I’m trying not to feel that feeling.\n\nAnd the mortgage folder — I know I’ve mentioned it, but that one is almost embarrassing because it’s just paperwork. It’s sitting there, and I’ll walk past it like it’s something alive. I can’t seem to open it because it pulls me right back to everything connected to that day. I’ll move it from one spot to another instead of actually looking at it.\n\nEven the herbs on my patio. That sounds so small, but I’ve been avoiding them too. I used to just step outside and water them, check on them, whatever. Now I see them through the door and I just turn away. Anything that feels tied to that day makes my body react before I can talk myself through it. My chest gets tight, and I just can’t make myself go near it.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding these situations or places because you're afraid of losing control of your emotions or reactions?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a big part of it. Like if I go to the house, or if I open that mortgage folder, or even if I step out to deal with the herbs on the patio, something in me just clamps down. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d call it a heart attack, exactly. I don’t know what that would feel like, and I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is. When it happens, my chest gets really tight and I get scared by how strong the reaction is, but I don’t think I’ve had the thought, like, “I’m dying” or “this is a heart attack.”

Passing out… I don’t know. I don’t think I’ve actually felt like I was going to faint. It’s more like I feel overwhelmed and trapped in the reaction, like I need to get away from whatever set it off before it gets worse. My body feels like it’s reacting before I can catch up, but I haven’t noticed it as, you know, blacking out or collapsing or anything like that. It’s mostly that tight chest feeling and the fear that I won’t be able to calm myself down.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.0, F43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I… I keep blowing up at people, and it’s scaring me a little. I work in retail, and I’m used to things being chaotic, people making mistakes, shelves being wrong, customers being difficult — that’s normal, I can usually handle that. But lately I’ve been snapping over things that honestly are not that big.\n\nLike the other day, one of my employees put some stock on the wrong shelf, and I just yelled. Not corrected them, not talked to them — I yelled. And afterward I was standing there shaking, like my whole body was buzzing, because that is not how I usually treat people. I kept thinking, “Why did I do that? Why was I so harsh?” It was such a small thing, and I reacted like it was some huge disaster. I don’t want to be that kind of person or that kind of manager. That’s basically why I came in — I feel like I can’t trust my reactions right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge more often than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much, honestly. I feel like my nerves are just… sitting right under my skin all the time.\n\nAt work especially, it’s embarrassing. If one of the store alarms beeps, or if someone bangs a pallet jack behind me, I jump so hard I almost drop whatever I’m holding. And it’s not just a little flinch, it’s like my whole body reacts before I even understand what the sound was. Then my heart keeps pounding afterward, even when everyone else has already gone back to what they were doing like nothing happened.\n\nI’ll be standing there trying to act normal, like, “Okay, keep scanning, keep stocking, keep talking,” but inside I’m still rattled. It feels like my body thinks something terrible just happened, even when it’s just a normal store noise.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts or urges to perform specific rituals that you feel compelled to follow?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? At least not in the way you mean, I don’t think.\n\nI mean, I do get stuck on things in my head sometimes, but it doesn’t feel like rituals, like I have to touch something a certain number of times or check the door over and over or wash my hands repeatedly. I’m not doing anything like that, as far as I’ve noticed.\n\nIf you mean do I keep replaying situations in my head, then maybe, but not because I feel like I have to do some specific action to make it stop. It’s more like my mind just won’t let something go. But no, I don’t have any particular routines or rules that I feel forced to follow.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know that I’d call it sad most of the time. Not exactly.\n\nI mean, I’m upset with myself a lot, and I feel worn down, but when you say sad or down, I picture crying all day or feeling hopeless, and I’m not sure that’s what this is. I can still get through work, I still do what I’m supposed to do, but I don’t feel like myself. It’s more like I’m tense and frustrated and then afterward I feel guilty, like, “What is wrong with me?”\n\nSo… maybe some down moments, yes, especially after I’ve snapped at someone or embarrassed myself, but I haven’t really been thinking of it as sadness most of the time. It feels more like I’m overwhelmed and not handling things the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain situations or places because of how they make you feel?\n\nPatient: Yes. I have been, and I didn’t even realize how much until I started noticing all the little ways I’m arranging my day around it.\n\nThe house is the big one. I keep finding excuses not to go near it, or not to deal with anything connected to it. Even just thinking about going over there makes my chest tighten, like my body is saying, “No, don’t.” It sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, because it’s just a place, but it doesn’t feel like just a place right now.\n\nAnd there’s this mortgage folder I need to deal with, papers and documents and all of that, and I can’t make myself open it. I’ll put it on the table and then move it somewhere else, or tell myself I’ll look at it after dinner, and then suddenly it’s bedtime and I haven’t touched it. I know it’s important. I know avoiding it probably makes things worse. But anything tied to that day just makes me feel like I can’t breathe right.\n\nEven the herbs on my patio… I’ve been avoiding those too. That sounds so small, I know, but I used to go out there and water them without thinking, and now I’ll see them through the glass and just turn away. It’s like they’re connected to all of it somehow, and my chest gets tight before I even step outside. I just can’t make myself go near things that remind me of what happened.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen that you're associating with the house or the things you're avoiding?\n\nPatient: Yes… something happened that day, and now it’s like my brain has attached everything around it to the house.\n\nI don’t even have to be there. Just thinking about the house, or seeing that mortgage folder sitting there, or even looking out at the herbs on my patio — things that should be totally ordinary — my chest tightens up and I just sort of freeze. I tell myself, “Just go deal with it, just open the folder, just water the plants,” and then I can’t. I move around it. I make excuses. I put it off until later.\n\nIt’s not that those things are dangerous by themselves. I know that logically. But they feel tied to that day, and once that feeling hits, I just want to get away from it. So I’ve been avoiding the house, the paperwork, even the patio, because anything connected to it makes me feel like I can’t get close to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself reliving that specific day or event as if it were happening again?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… not like I’m literally standing there every second, but it comes in so fast that it feels like I’m back in it before I even understand what’s happening.\n\nThe fire just jumps into my head out of nowhere. I can be doing something completely normal, like picking up a pan in the kitchen, and suddenly it’s not just a pan anymore — I’m smelling smoke again, and I can see the kitchen filling up, like that moment is happening right in front of me. And it’s so vivid for a few seconds that I’m not thinking, “Oh, this is a memory.” I’m just back there, trying to make sense of it and stop it, and then I realize I’m standing in my own kitchen now, or wherever I am, and I feel shaken.\n\nIt’s happened with things that don’t even seem connected at first. Like I’ll open a spreadsheet at work, just some ordinary thing I need to do, and then my mind will suddenly throw me right back to that day — the smoke, the kitchen, that awful feeling of everything happening too fast. I hate it because there’s no warning. I’ll be fine, or at least trying to be fine, and then there it is, like my brain yanks me back into it before I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other places, activities, or things that you've been avoiding because they remind you of the fire?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly the same few things, but they sort of spread out into my day more than I want to admit.\n\nI avoid the house the most. If I have to go near it or even think about dealing with something there, I start feeling it in my chest right away, like it tightens before I’ve even made a decision. I don’t think I feel like I’m going to die, no. Not in those words, anyway. I don’t usually have that thought of, “This is it,” or “I’m dying.” It’s more confusing than that, because my body reacts so strongly that it scares me, but my mind doesn’t quite go to death.

It’s like they’re connected to all of it somehow, and my chest gets tight before I even step outside. Just thinking about the house, or seeing that mortgage folder sitting there, or even looking out at the herbs on my patio — things that should be totally ordinary — my chest tightens up and I just sort of freeze. I tell myself, “Just go deal with it, just open the folder, just water the plants,” and then I can’t. I move around it. I make excuses. I put it off until later.\n\nIt’s not that those things are dangerous by themselves. I know that logically. But they feel tied to that day, and once that feeling hits, I just want to get away from it. So I’ve been avoiding the house, the paperwork, even the patio, because anything connected to it makes me feel like I can’t get close to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself reliving that specific day or event as if it were happening again?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… not like I’m literally standing there every second, but it comes in so fast that it feels like I’m back in it before I even understand what’s happening.\n\nThe fire just jumps into my head out of nowhere. I can be doing something completely normal, like picking up a pan in the kitchen, and suddenly it’s not just a pan anymore — I’m smelling smoke again, and I can see the kitchen filling up, like that moment is happening right in front of me. And it’s so vivid for a few seconds that I’m not thinking, “Oh, this is a memory.” I’m just back there, trying to make sense of it and stop it, and then I realize I’m standing in my own kitchen now, or wherever I am, and I feel shaken.\n\nIt’s happened with things that don’t even seem connected at first. Like I’ll open a spreadsheet at work, just some ordinary thing I need to do, and then my mind will suddenly throw me right back to that day — the smoke, the kitchen, that awful feeling of everything happening too fast. I hate it because there’s no warning. I’ll be fine, or at least trying to be fine, and then there it is, like my brain yanks me back into it before I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other places, activities, or things that you've been avoiding because they remind you of the fire?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly the same few things, but they sort of spread out into my day more than I want to admit.\n\nI avoid the house the most. If I have to go near it or even think about dealing with something there, I start feeling it in my chest right away, like it tightens before I’ve even made a decision. I’ll tell myself I’m just too busy, or that I’ll go later, or that someone else can handle it, but really I know I’m trying not to feel that feeling.\n\nAnd the mortgage folder — I know I’ve mentioned it, but that one is almost embarrassing because it’s just paperwork. It’s sitting there, and I’ll walk past it like it’s something alive. I can’t seem to open it because it pulls me right back to everything connected to that day. I’ll move it from one spot to another instead of actually looking at it.\n\nEven the herbs on my patio. That sounds so small, but I’ve been avoiding them too. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I think it’s anything that connects back to that day, even if it’s not… I don’t know, even if it’s not obviously dangerous now.

The house is the biggest one. Just thinking about going near it, or having to deal with anything related to it, I feel my chest tighten almost immediately. And then there’s the mortgage folder, which sounds so ordinary, but it’s sitting there and I know what’s in it, and I know it means dealing with the house and everything that happened, so I just avoid opening it. I’ll walk past it like it’s something hot.

And even the herbs on my patio, which feels ridiculous to say out loud, because they’re just herbs, but they’re tied to that day in my head somehow. I see them and it brings me right back to everything around the fire, and I just can’t make myself go near them. It’s like my body decides before I do. My chest tightens, and then I back away or distract myself or tell myself I’ll handle it later.

So yes, I think it’s the connection to that day. Anything that feels linked to the fire, even in a small way, sets off that reaction in me.

Not exactly.\n\nI mean, I’m upset with myself a lot, and I feel worn down, but when you say sad or down, I picture crying all day or feeling hopeless, and I’m not sure that’s what this is. I can still get through work, I still do what I’m supposed to do, but I don’t feel like myself. It’s more like I’m tense and frustrated and then afterward I feel guilty, like, “What is wrong with me?”\n\nSo… maybe some down moments, yes, especially after I’ve snapped at someone or embarrassed myself, but I haven’t really been thinking of it as sadness most of the time. It feels more like I’m overwhelmed and not handling things the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain situations or places because of how they make you feel?\n\nPatient: Yes. I have been, and I didn’t even realize how much until I started noticing all the little ways I’m arranging my day around it.\n\nThe house is the big one. I keep finding excuses not to go near it, or not to deal with anything connected to it. Even just thinking about going over there makes my chest tighten, like my body is saying, “No, don’t.” It sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, because it’s just a place, but it doesn’t feel like just a place right now.\n\nAnd there’s this mortgage folder I need to deal with, papers and documents and all of that, and I can’t make myself open it. I’ll put it on the table and then move it somewhere else, or tell myself I’ll look at it after dinner, and then suddenly it’s bedtime and I haven’t touched it. I know it’s important. I know avoiding it probably makes things worse. But anything tied to that day just makes me feel like I can’t breathe right.\n\nEven the herbs on my patio… I’ve been avoiding those too. That sounds so small, I know, but I used to go out there and water them without thinking, and now I’ll see them through the glass and just turn away. It’s like they’re connected to all of it somehow, and my chest gets tight before I even step outside. I just can’t make myself go near things that remind me of what happened.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen that you're associating with the house or the things you're avoiding?\n\nPatient: Yes… something happened that day, and now it’s like my brain has attached everything around it to the house.\n\nI don’t even have to be there. Just thinking about the house, or seeing that mortgage folder sitting there, or even looking out at the herbs on my patio — things that should be totally ordinary — my chest tightens up and I just sort of freeze. I tell myself, “Just go deal with it, just open the folder, just water the plants,” and then I can’t. I move around it. I make excuses. I put it off until later.\n\nIt’s not that those things are dangerous by themselves. I know that logically. But they feel tied to that day, and once that feeling hits, I just want to get away from it. So I’ve been avoiding the house, the paperwork, even the patio, because anything connected to it makes me feel like I can’t get close to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself reliving that specific day or event as if it were happening again?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… not like I’m literally standing there every second, but it comes in so fast that it feels like I’m back in it before I even understand what’s happening.\n\nThe fire just jumps into my head out of nowhere. I can be doing something completely normal, like picking up a pan in the kitchen, and suddenly it’s not just a pan anymore — I’m smelling smoke again, and I can see the kitchen filling up, like that moment is happening right in front of me. And it’s so vivid for a few seconds that I’m not thinking, “Oh, this is a memory.” I’m just back there, trying to make sense of it and stop it, and then I realize I’m standing in my own kitchen now, or wherever I am, and I feel shaken.\n\nIt’s happened with things that don’t even seem connected at first. Like I’ll open a spreadsheet at work, just some ordinary thing I need to do, and then my mind will suddenly throw me right back to that day — the smoke, the kitchen, that awful feeling of everything happening too fast. I hate it because there’s no warning. I’ll be fine, or at least trying to be fine, and then there it is, like my brain yanks me back into it before I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other places, activities, or things that you've been avoiding because they remind you of the fire?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly the same few things, but they sort of spread out into my day more than I want to admit.\n\nI avoid the house the most. If I have to go near it or even think about dealing with something there, I start feeling it in my chest right away, like it tightens before I’ve even made a decision. I’ll tell myself I’m just too busy, or that I’ll go later, or that someone else can handle it, but really I know I’m trying not to feel that feeling.\n\nAnd the mortgage folder — I know I’ve mentioned it, but that one is almost embarrassing because it’s just paperwork. It’s sitting there, and I’ll walk past it like it’s something alive. I can’t seem to open it because it pulls me right back to everything connected to that day. I’ll move it from one spot to another instead of actually looking at it.\n\nEven the herbs on my patio. That sounds so small, but I’ve been avoiding them too. I used to just step outside and water them, check on them, whatever. Now I see them through the door and I just turn away. Anything that feels tied to that day makes my body react before I can talk myself through it. My chest gets tight, and I just can’t make myself go near it.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding these situations or places because you're afraid of losing control of your emotions or reactions?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a big part of it. It’s not like I sit there and calmly decide, “I’m afraid I’ll lose control,” but that’s what it feels like underneath. Like if I go to the house, or if I open that mortgage folder, or even if I step out to deal with the herbs on the patio, something in me just clamps down. My chest gets tight right away, and I feel like if I keep going toward it, I’m going to get overwhelmed and not be able to pull myself back together.\n\nSo I avoid it. I walk around the folder. I look away from the patio. I put off anything connected to the house. And I know those things aren’t the fire itself, I know they’re just objects or places, but they’re tied to that day in my mind, and once that feeling starts, I just… I can’t make myself go near them. It feels safer to stay away than to risk having that reaction take over.\n\nDoctor: When you have these intense reactions, do you ever feel like you're having a heart attack or going to pass out?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it a heart attack, exactly. I don’t know what that would feel like, and I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is. When it happens, my chest gets really tight and I get scared by how strong the reaction is, but I don’t think I’ve had the thought, like, “I’m dying” or “this is a heart attack.”\n\nPassing out… I don’t know. I don’t think I’ve actually felt like I was going to faint. It’s more like I feel overwhelmed and trapped in the reaction, like I need to get away from whatever set it off before it gets worse. My body feels like it’s reacting before I can catch up, but I haven’t noticed it as, you know, blacking out or collapsing or anything like that. It’s mostly that tight chest feeling and the fear that I won’t be able to calm myself down.\n\nDoctor: When you have these intense reactions, do you feel like you're going to die or lose control?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I feel like I’m going to die, no. Not in those words, anyway. I don’t usually have that thought of, “This is it,” or “I’m dying.” It’s more confusing than that, because my body reacts so strongly that it scares me, but my mind doesn’t quite go to death.\n\nLosing control is harder to answer. I don’t know if I mean losing control like screaming or doing something dramatic. It’s more that I’m afraid I won’t be able to stop the feeling once it starts. Like if I let myself go near the thing, or stay with it too long, I might fall apart emotionally and not be able to pull myself back. So I guess not losing control in a dangerous way, but losing control of my reaction, maybe. I’m not totally sure how to explain it.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the fire or that day that you think triggers these intense reactions in you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think it’s anything that connects back to that day, even if it’s not… I don’t know, even if it’s not obviously dangerous now.\n\nThe house is the biggest one. Just thinking about going near it, or having to deal with anything related to it, I feel my chest tighten almost immediately. And then there’s the mortgage folder, which sounds so ordinary, but it’s sitting there and I know what’s in it, and I know it means dealing with the house and everything that happened, so I just avoid opening it. I’ll walk past it like it’s something hot.\n\nAnd even the herbs on my patio, which feels ridiculous to say out loud, because they’re just herbs, but they’re tied to that day in my head somehow. I see them and it brings me right back to everything around the fire, and I just can’t make myself go near them. It’s like my body decides before I do. My chest tightens, and then I back away or distract myself or tell myself I’ll handle it later.\n\nSo yes, I think it’s the connection to that day. Anything that feels linked to the fire, even in a small way, sets off that reaction in me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I think that is happening. I don’t know if I would have called it “reliving” at first, because it’s not like I decide to sit and remember it. It just sort of jumps into my head without warning.

Like I’ll be doing something completely normal, something that shouldn’t be emotional at all, and suddenly it’s there. I might pick up a pan in the kitchen, or open a spreadsheet at work, and then before I can even tell myself not to think about it, I’m back in that moment. I can smell smoke, or I can see the kitchen filling up, and it feels so immediate. Not like a regular memory where you’re looking back on something from a distance, more like my body believes it’s happening again for a few seconds.

And it’s the sensory part that really gets me. The smell, the look of it, the feeling of that moment rushing back. I’ll try to shake it off and tell myself I’m here, I’m not there, but it comes in so fast. It’s upsetting because there isn’t always an obvious reason for it. I can be doing paperwork or something totally unrelated, and then suddenly I’m right back with the fire in my head.
